Learning Deterministic Policies with Policy Gradients in Constrained Markov Decision Processes
Alessandro Montenegro, Leonardo Cesani, Marco Mussi, Matteo Papini, Alberto Maria Metelli

Abstract
Constrained Reinforcement Learning (CRL) addresses sequential decision-making problems where agents are required to achieve goals by maximizing the expected return while meeting domain-specific constraints. In this setting, policy-based methods are widely used thanks to their advantages when dealing with continuous-control problems. These methods search in the policy space with an action-based or a parameter-based exploration strategy, depending on whether they learn the parameters of a stochastic policy or those of a stochastic hyperpolicy. We introduce an exploration-agnostic algorithm, called C-PG , which enjoys global last-iterate convergence guarantees under gradient domination assumptions. Furthermore, under specific noise models where the (hyper)policy is expressed as a stochastic perturbation of the actions or of the parameters of an underlying deterministic policy, we additionally establish global last-iterate convergence guarantees of C-PG to the optimal deterministic policy . This holds when learning a stochastic (hyper)policy and subsequently switching off the stochasticity at the end of training, thereby deploying a deterministic policy. Finally, we empirically validate both the action-based ( C-PGAE ) and parameter-based ( C-PGPE ) variants of C-PG on constrained control tasks, and compare them against state-of-the-art baselines, demonstrating their effectiveness, in particular when deploying deterministic policies after training. 1 1 1 This work extends the preliminary version presented in ( Montenegro et al. 2024b ) by providing a theoretical analysis and empirical evaluation of deterministic policy deployment.

1
Introduction

When applying Reinforcement Learning
(
Sutton and Barto 2018
, RL,)
to real-world scenarios, we aim at solving large-scale continuous control problems where, in addition to reaching a goal, it is necessary to meet structural or utility-based constraints.
For instance, an autonomous-driving car has its main objective of getting to the desired destination (i.e., goal) while avoiding collisions, ensuring the safety of people on the streets, adhering to traffic rules, and respecting the physical requirements of the engine to avoid damaging it (i.e., constraints)
(
Likmeta et al. 2020
)
.
To pursue such an objective, it is necessary to extend the RL problem formulation with the possibility to account for constraints.
Constrained Reinforcement Learning
(
Uchibe and Doya 2007
, CRL,)
aims at solving this family of problems by employing RL techniques to tackle Constrained Markov Decision Processes
(
Altman 1999
, CMDPs,)
, which provide an established and widely-used framework for modeling constrained control tasks.
The conventional CRL framework primarily focuses on constraints related directly to
expected costs

(
Stooke et al. 2020
;
Ding et al. 2020
;
Ying et al. 2022
;
Ding et al. 2024
)
.

Among the RL methods applicable to CMDPs, Policy Gradients
(
Deisenroth et al. 2013
, PGs,)
are particularly appealing.
Indeed, PGs have demonstrably achieved impressive results in continuous-control problems due to several advantages that make them well-suited for real-world applications.
These advantages include the ability to handle continuous state and action spaces
(
Peters and Schaal 2006
)
, resilience to sensor and actuator noise
(
Gravell et al. 2020
)
, robustness in partially-observable environments
(
Azizzadenesheli et al. 2018
)
, and the possibility of incorporating expert knowledge during the policy design phase
(
Ghavamzadeh and Engel 2006
)
, thus improving the efficacy, safety, and interpretability of the learned policy
(
Likmeta et al. 2020
)
.
PGs can be categorized into two key families depending on the way exploration is carried out in the policy space
(
Montenegro et al. 2024a
)
. Following their taxonomy, we distinguish between the
action-based
(AB) and the
parameter-based
(PB) exploration paradigms. The former, employed by
REINFORCE

(
Williams 1992
)
and
GPOMDP

(
Baxter and Bartlett 2001
)
, focuses on directly learning the parameters of a parametric stochastic
policy
. The latter, employed by
PGPE

(
Sehnke et al. 2010
)
, is tasked with learning the parameters of a parametric stochastic
hyperpolicy
from which the parameters of the actual policy (often deterministic) are sampled.

PGs have gained significant popularity in solving constrained control problems
(
Achiam et al. 2017
)
.
Within this field, algorithms are primarily developed using
primal-dual
methods
(
Chow et al. 2017
;
Tessler et al. 2019
;
Ding et al. 2020
;
Ding et al. 2021
;
Bai et al. 2022
)
, which can be formulated through
Lagrangian optimization
of the primal (i.e., policy or hyperpolicy parameters) and dual variable (i.e., Lagrange multipliers).
Even though the distinction between the exploration paradigms is well known in the PG methods literature, the current state of the art in policy-based CRL focuses only on the
action-based
exploration approach
(
Achiam et al. 2017
;
Stooke et al. 2020
;
Bai et al. 2023
)
, while the
parameter-based
one remains unexplored
(
Montenegro et al. 2024b
)
.
A critical challenge for policy-based Lagrangian optimization algorithms is ensuring convergence guarantees. Existing works have spent a notable effort in this direction
(
Ying et al. 2022
;
Gladin et al. 2023
;
Ding et al. 2024
)
.
Recently,
Ying et al. 2022
,
Gladin et al. 2023
, and
Ding et al. 2024
manage to ensure
global last-iterate
convergence guarantees. However, these approaches are affected by some notable limitations: (
i
) the provided convergence rates depend on the cardinality of the state and action spaces, limiting their applicability to tabular CMPDs and preventing scaling to realistic continuous control problems; (
ii
) they focus on
softmax
policies only, disregarding other more realistic policy models (e.g., Gaussian ones); (
iii
) ensure convergence when a single constraint only is present
(
Ding et al. 2024
;
Rozada et al. 2025
)
.

Real-world problems not only require RL algorithms to produce policies satisfying constraints, but they also often demand the resulting policy to be deterministic to meet reliability, safety, and traceability requirements. To this end, PGs remain a recommended choice.
Considering unconstrained scenarios, the challenge of learning deterministic policies was first addressed by
(
Silver et al. 2014
)
, that introduced the
deterministic policy gradient
(DPG) method, later inspiring successful deep RL algorithms such as DDPG
(
Lillicrap et al. 2015
;
Fujimoto et al. 2018
)
. However, DPG-based approaches present notable drawbacks due to their off-policy nature, which makes the theoretical analysis complex and limits local convergence guarantees to restrictive assumptions
(
Xiong et al. 2022
)
.
More recently,
Montenegro et al. 2024a
proposed a unified framework for deterministic policy deployment that bridges action-based and parameter-based exploration paradigms. Their approach is grounded on specific noise models that represent stochastic policies and hyperpolicies as
perturbations
of the actions or the parameters of an underlying deterministic policy. The core idea is to
train stochastic
(hyper)policies via policy gradient algorithms and
deploy
their
deterministic
counterparts by
switching off
the stochasticity, thereby offering a principled methodology for incorporating deterministic policies within the policy gradient framework.
The latter contribution is a recent advancement in the unconstrained setting, while in the constrained one, despite the advancements in policy-based CRL, the integration of deterministic policies within this framework remains mostly unexplored.
A recent contribution in this direction is presented by
Rozada et al. 2025
, who introduce the
Deterministic Policy Gradient Primal-Dual
(D-PGPD) algorithm, a novel approach designed to directly learn deterministic policies in CMDPs with continuous state and action spaces. D-PGPD is a primal-dual algorithm that incorporates entropy regularization w.r.t. the policy and ridge regularization w.r.t. the dual variable.
Unlike traditional stochastic policy-based CRL methods, which introduce exploration through policy randomness, D-PGPD learns a fully deterministic policy and relies solely on the inherent stochasticity of the environment. While this design ensures stable and consistent policy execution, it may face limitations in environments where intrinsic stochasticity is insufficient to explore the state-action space effectively.
Moreover, the algorithm is developed for single-constraint settings, which may reduce its applicability to real-world problems involving multiple constraints. In the considered setup, and under additional assumptions, such as boundedness of the action space, the sample-based version of D-PGPD requires a sample complexity of order
\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{-18})
to the optimal feasible deterministic policy in the last iterate. This unsatisfactory rate highlights the need for further research on more efficient methods.

Original Contribution.

The goal of this work is to introduce a framework for solving constrained continuous control problems using policy-based primal-dual algorithms that operate in both the
action-based
and
parameter-based
policy gradient exploration scenarios, while providing global last-iterate convergence guarantees with general (hyper)policy parameterization to both optimal stochastic (hyper)policies and deterministic policies. Specifically, the main contributions of this work can be summarized as follows:

•

In Section
2
, we introduce a general constrained optimization problem, which is agnostic w.r.t. both the
action-based
and
parameter-based
exploration paradigm.

•

In Section
3
, we introduce
C-PG
, a general policy-based primal-dual algorithm optimizing the regularized Lagrangian function associated with the general constrained optimization problem shown in Section
2
. We show that, under (weak) gradient domination assumptions, it simultaneously achieves the following: (
i
)
last-iterate
convergence guarantees to a globally optimal feasible policy (i.e., satisfying all the constraints); (
ii
) compatibility with CMDPs having
continuous state and action spaces
; (
iii
) the ability to handle
multiple constraints
.

•

In Section
4
, we restrict action-based and parameter-based exploration paradigms as white-noise perturbations applied to the actions or parameters of an underlying parametric deterministic policy. Based on this characterization, we define
deterministic deployment
as the process of
switching off
the noise
(
Montenegro et al. 2024a
)
in the learned stochastic (hyper)policies. Within this framework, we derive all the conditions required to ensure the last-iterate global convergence of
C-PG
, as presented in Section
3
. Finally, we show that this approach guarantees last-iterate convergence to the optimal deterministic policy in the constrained setting.

In Section
6
, we numerically validate the parameter-based and the action-based variants of
C-PG
against state-of-the-art baselines in constrained control problems.
Related work is discussed in Section
5
.
Omitted proofs and additional technical results are reported in Appendix
A
.

2
Preliminaries

In this section, we present the notation we will use throughout this manuscript and the preliminaries needed to understand its content. Moreover, after having introduced the the
AB
and
PB
exploration paradigms, we introduce the exploration-agnostic constrained optimization problem we aim at solving with the introduced method.

Notation.

For a measurable set
\mathcal{X}
, we denote as
\Delta(\mathcal{X})
the set of probability measures over
\mathcal{X}
. For
P\in\Delta(\mathcal{X})
, we denote with
p
its density function w.r.t. a reference measure that we assume to exist whenever needed. With a little abuse of notation, we will interchangeably use
x\sim P
or
x\sim p
to express that random variable
x
is distributed according to
P
.
For
n,m\in\mathbb{N}
with
n\leq m
, we denote
\mathopen{[\![}n\mathclose{]\!]}\coloneqq\left\{1,2,\ldots,n\right\}
and with
\mathopen{[\![}n,m\mathclose{]\!]}\coloneqq\left\{n,n+1,\ldots,m\right\}
.
For a vector
\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we denote as
x_{i}
the
i
-th component of
\bm{x}
.
For
a\in\mathbb{R}
, we define
(a)^{+}\coloneqq\max\{0,a\}
and we extend the notation to vectors as
(\bm{x})^{+}=((x_{1})^{+},\dots,(x_{d})^{+})^{\top}
. Given a set
\mathcal{X}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we denote with
\Pi_{\mathcal{X}}
the Euclidean-norm projection, i.e.,
\Pi_{\mathcal{X}}\bm{x}\in\argmin_{\bm{y}\in\mathcal{X}}\|\bm{y}-\bm{x}\|_{2}
for any
\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. For two vectors
\bm{x},\bm{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we denote with
\langle\bm{x},\bm{y}\rangle
their inner product. A function
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is
L_{1}
-Lipschitz Continuous (
L_{1}
-LC) if
|f(\bm{x})-f(\bm{x}^{\prime})|\leq L_{1}\|\bm{x}-\bm{x}^{\prime}\|_{2}
and
L_{2}
-Lipschitz Smooth (
L_{2}
-LS) if it is differentiable and
\|\nabla_{\bm{x}}f(\bm{x})-\nabla_{\bm{x}}f(\bm{x}^{\prime})\|_{2}\leq L_{2}\|\bm{x}-\bm{x}^{\prime}\|_{2}
for every
\bm{x},\bm{x}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Constrained Markov Decision Processes.

A Constrained Markov Decision Process
(
Altman 1999
, CMDP,)
with
U\in\mathbb{N}
constraints is represented by
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{C}}\coloneqq\left(\mathcal{S},\mathcal{A},p,r,\{c_{i}\}_{i\in\mathopen{[\![}U\mathclose{]\!]}},\{b_{i}\}_{i\in\mathopen{[\![}U\mathclose{]\!]}},\phi_{0},\gamma\right)
, where
\mathcal{S}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathcal{S}}}
and
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathcal{A}}}
are the measurable state and action spaces;
p:\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\to\Delta\left(\mathcal{S}\right)
is the transition model, where
p(\bm{s}^{\prime}|\bm{s},\bm{a})
is the probability density of getting to state
\bm{s}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}
given that action
\bm{a}\in\mathcal{A}
is taken in state
\bm{s}\in\mathcal{S}
;
r:\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\to[-1,0]
is the reward function, where
r(\bm{s},\bm{a})
is the instantaneous reward obtained by playing action
\bm{a}
in state
\bm{s}
;
c_{i}:\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{A}\to[0,1]
is the
i
-th cost function, where
c_{i}(\bm{s},\bm{a})
is the
i
-th instantaneous cost obtained by playing action
\bm{a}
in state
\bm{s}
;
b_{i}\in[0,J_{\max}]
is the threshold for the
i
-th cost for every
i\in\mathopen{[\![}U\mathclose{]\!]}
;
\phi_{0}\in\Delta(\mathcal{S})
is the initial state distribution; and
\gamma\in[0,1]
is the discount factor.
A trajectory
\tau
of length
T\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{+\infty\}
2
2

2

We admit
\gamma=1
just when
T<+\infty
.
is a sequence of
T
state-action pairs:
\tau=\left(\bm{s}_{\tau,0},\bm{a}_{\tau,0},\dots,\bm{s}_{\tau,T-1},\bm{a}_{\tau,T-1}\right)
.
The
discounted return
over a trajectory
\tau
is
R(\tau)\coloneqq\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\gamma^{t}r(\bm{s}_{\tau,t},\bm{a}_{\tau,t})
, while the
i
-th
discounted cumulative cost
is
C_{i}(\tau)\coloneqq\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\gamma^{t}c_{i}(\bm{s}_{\tau,t},\bm{a}_{\tau,t})
. We define the additional cost function
c_{0}(\bm{s},\bm{a})\coloneqq-r(\bm{s},\bm{a})\in[0,1]
and
C_{0}(\tau)\coloneqq-R(\tau)
just for presentation purposes. Note that, with
J_{\max}\coloneqq\frac{1-\gamma^{T}}{1-\gamma}
,
R(\tau)\in[-J_{\max},0]
and
C_{i}(\tau)\in[0,J_{\max}]
, for every
i\in\mathopen{[\![}U\mathclose{]\!]}
and trajectory
\tau
. Our goal is to minimize
\E[C_{0}(\tau)]
subject to the constraints
\E[C_{i}(\tau)]\leq b_{i}
for every
i\in\mathopen{[\![}U\mathclose{]\!]}
.

Action-based Policy Gradients.

Action-based (AB) PG methods focus on learning the parameters
\bm{\theta}\in\Theta\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d_{\Theta}}
of a parametric stochastic policy
\pi_{\bm{\theta}}:\mathcal{S}\to\Delta(\mathcal{A})
, where
\pi_{\bm{\theta}}(\bm{a}|\bm{s})
represents the probability density of selecting action
\bm{a}\in\mathcal{A}
being in state
\bm{s}\in\mathcal{S}
.
At each step
t
of the interaction with the environment, the stochastic policy is employed to sample an action
\bm{a}_{t}\sim\pi_{\bm{\theta}_{t}}(\cdot|\bm{s}_{t})
.
To assess the performance of
\pi_{\bm{\theta}}
w.r.t. the
i
-th cost function, with
i\in\mathopen{[\![}0,U\mathclose{]\!]}
, we employ the
AB performance index

{\color[rgb]{0,0.4648,0.7344}J_{\text{A},i}}:\Theta\to\mathbb{R}
, which is defined as
{\color[rgb]{0,0.4648,0.7344}J_{\text{A},i}}(\bm{\theta})\coloneqq\E_{\tau\sim p_{\text{A}}(\cdot|\bm{\theta})}\left[C_{i}(\tau)\right]
,
where
p_{\text{A}}(\tau,\bm{\theta})\coloneqq\phi_{0}(\bm{s}_{\tau,0})\prod_{t=0}^{T-1}\pi_{\bm{\theta}}(\bm{a}_{\tau,t}|\bm{s}_{\tau,t})p(\bm{s}_{\tau,t+1}|\bm{s}_{\tau,t},\bm{a}_{\tau,t})
is the density of trajectory
\tau
induced by policy
\pi_{\bm{\theta}}
.

Parameter-based Policy Gradients.

Parameter-based (PB) PG methods focus on learning the parameters
\bm{\rho}\in\mathcal{R}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathcal{R}}}
of a parametric stochastic hyperpolicy
\nu_{\bm{\rho}}\in\Delta(\Theta)
.
The hyperpolicy
\nu_{\bm{\rho}}
is used to sample parameter configurations
\bm{\theta}\sim\nu_{\bm{\rho}}
to be plugged into an underlying parametric policy
\pi_{\bm{\theta}}
, that will be then used for the interaction with the environment. Notice that
\pi_{\bm{\theta}}
can also be deterministic.
To assess the performance of
\nu_{\bm{\rho}}
w.r.t. the
i
-th cost function, with
i\in\mathopen{[\![}0,U\mathclose{]\!]}
, we employ the
PB performance index

{\color[rgb]{0.8008,0.1992,0.0664}J_{\text{P},i}}:\mathcal{R}\to\mathbb{R}
, which is defined as
{\color[rgb]{0.8008,0.1992,0.0664}J_{\text{P},i}}(\bm{\rho})\coloneqq\E_{\bm{\theta}\sim\nu_{\bm{\rho}}}\left[\E_{\tau\sim p_{\text{A}}(\cdot|\bm{\theta})}\left[C_{i}(\tau)\right]\right]
.

Constrained Optimization Problem.

Having introduced the
AB
and
PB
performance indices, we formulate a
constrained optimization problem
(COP), which is agnostic w.r.t. the exploration paradigm:

\displaystyle\min_{\bm{\upsilon}\in\mathcal{V}}J_{\dagger,0}(\bm{\upsilon})\quad\text{s.t.}\quad J_{\dagger,i}(\bm{\upsilon})\leq b_{i},\;\;\forall i\in\mathopen{[\![}U\mathclose{]\!]},

(1)

where
\dagger\in\{\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.4648,0.7344}A}},\text{{\color[rgb]{0.8008,0.1992,0.0664}P}}\}
and
\bm{\upsilon}
is a generic parameter vector belonging to the parameter space
\mathcal{V}
.
When
\dagger=\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.4648,0.7344}A}}
, we are considering the
AB
exploration paradigm, so
\mathcal{V}=\Theta
.
On the other hand, when
\dagger=\text{{\color[rgb]{0.8008,0.1992,0.0664}P}}
, we are in the
PB
exploration paradigm, thus
\mathcal{V}=\mathcal{R}
.

3
Last-Iterate Global Convergence of
C-PG

In this section, we present
C-PG
, a general primal-dual algorithm that optimizes a regularized version of the Lagrangian function (Section
3.1
) associated with the COP of Equation (
1
). After having introduced the necessary assumptions (Section
3.2
), we show that
C-PG
exhibit
dimension-free
3
3

3

The
dimension-free
property
(
Liu et al. 2021
;
Ding et al. 2020
;
Ding et al. 2022
;
Ding et al. 2024
)
is achieved when the convergence rates do not depend on the cardinality of the state and/or action spaces.

last-iterate global
convergence guarantees (Section
3.3
).
For notational convenience, in the rest of this section, we use
J_{i}
in place of
J_{\dagger,i}
.

3.1
Regularized Lagrangian Approach

To solve the COP of Equation (
1
) we resort to the method of Lagrange multipliers
(
Bertsekas 2014
)
introducing the Lagrangian function
\mathcal{L}_{0}(\bm{\upsilon},\bm{\lambda})\coloneqq J_{0}(\bm{\upsilon})+\sum_{i=1}^{U}\lambda_{i}\left(J_{i}(\bm{\upsilon})-b_{i}\right)=J_{0}(\bm{\upsilon})+\langle\bm{\lambda},\mathbf{J}(\bm{\upsilon})-\mathbf{b}\rangle
, where
\bm{\upsilon}\in\mathcal{V}
is the primal variable and
\bm{\lambda}{\in\mathbb{R}^{U}_{\geq 0}}
are the Lagrangian multipliers or dual variable,
\mathbf{J}=(J_{1},\dots,J_{U})^{\top}
, and
\mathbf{b}=(b_{1},\dots,b_{U})^{\top}
. This allows rephrasing the COP in Equation (
1
) as a min-max optimization problem
\min_{\bm{\upsilon}\in\mathcal{V}}\max_{\bm{\lambda}{\in\mathbb{R}^{U}_{\geq 0}}}\mathcal{L}_{0}(\bm{\upsilon},\bm{\lambda})
and we denote with
H_{0}(\bm{\upsilon})\coloneqq\max_{\bm{\lambda}{\in\mathbb{R}^{U}_{\geq 0}}}\mathcal{L}_{0}(\bm{\upsilon},\bm{\lambda})
the
primal function
and its optimum with
H^{*}_{0}\coloneqq\min_{\bm{\upsilon}\in\mathcal{V}}H_{0}(\bm{\upsilon})
.
To obtain a
last-iterate
convergence guarantee, we make use of a regularization approach. Specifically, let
\omega>0
be a regularization parameter, we define the
\omega
-regularized Lagrangian function
as follows:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\omega}(\bm{\upsilon},\bm{\lambda})\coloneqq J_{0}(\bm{\upsilon})+\sum_{i=1}^{U}\lambda_{i}\left(J_{i}(\bm{\upsilon})-b_{i}\right)-\frac{\omega}{2}\left\|\bm{\lambda}\right\|_{2}^{2}=J_{0}(\bm{\upsilon})+\langle\bm{\lambda},\mathbf{J}(\bm{\upsilon})-\mathbf{b}\rangle-\frac{\omega}{2}\left\|\bm{\lambda}\right\|_{2}^{2}=\mathcal{L}_{0}(\bm{\upsilon},\bm{\lambda})-\frac{\omega}{2}\left\|\bm{\lambda}\right\|_{2}^{2}.

The ridge regularization makes
\mathcal{L}_{\omega}(\bm{\upsilon},\bm{\lambda})
a strongly concave function of
\bm{\lambda}
at the price of a bias that is quantified in Lemmas
A.1
,
A.2
, and
A.3
. Thus, we address the
\omega
-regularized min-max optimization problem
\min_{\bm{\upsilon}\in\mathcal{V}}\max_{\bm{\lambda}{\in\Lambda}}\mathcal{L}_{\omega}(\bm{\upsilon},\bm{\lambda})
, where
\Lambda\coloneqq\{\bm{\lambda}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{U}\,:\,\|\bm{\lambda}\|_{2}\leq\omega^{-1}\sqrt{U}J_{\max}\}
, in replacement of the original (non-regularized) one.
We stress that this choice of
\Lambda
guarantees that the optimal Lagrange multipliers
\bm{\lambda}_{\omega}^{*}
lie within
\Lambda
.
For this problem, we introduce the primal function
H_{\omega}(\bm{\upsilon})\coloneqq\max_{\bm{\lambda}{\in\Lambda}}\mathcal{L}_{\omega}(\bm{\upsilon},\bm{\lambda})
, that, thanks to the ridge regularization, admits the closed-form expression:

\displaystyle H_{\omega}(\bm{\upsilon})=J_{0}(\bm{\upsilon})+\frac{1}{2\omega}\sum_{i=1}^{U}\left(\left(J_{i}(\bm{\upsilon})-b_{i}\right)^{+}\right)^{2}=J_{0}(\bm{\upsilon})+\frac{1}{2\omega}\|(\mathbf{J}(\bm{\upsilon})-\mathbf{b})^{+}\|^{2}_{2},

where the optimal values of the Lagrange multipliers are given by:

\displaystyle\bm{\lambda}^{*}(\bm{\upsilon})=\Pi_{\Lambda}\left(\frac{1}{\omega}(\mathbf{J}(\bm{\upsilon})-\mathbf{b})\right)=\frac{1}{\omega}(\mathbf{J}(\bm{\upsilon})-\mathbf{b})^{+},

that is guaranteed to have norm smaller than
\omega^{-1}\sqrt{U}J_{\max}
. Furthermore, we define
H^{*}_{\omega}\coloneqq\min_{\bm{\upsilon}\in\mathcal{V}}H_{\omega}(\bm{\upsilon})
.
C-PG
updates the parameters
(\bm{\upsilon}_{k},\bm{\lambda}_{k})
with an
alternate gradient descent-ascent
scheme for every iterate
k\in\mathbb{N}
:

Primal Update
:

\bm{\upsilon}_{k+1}\leftarrow\Pi_{\mathcal{V}}\left(\bm{\upsilon}_{k}-\zeta_{\bm{\upsilon},k}\widehat{\nabla}_{\bm{\upsilon}}\mathcal{L}_{\omega}(\bm{\upsilon}_{k},\bm{\lambda}_{k})\right),

Dual Update
:

\bm{\lambda}_{k+1}\leftarrow\Pi_{\Lambda}\left(\bm{\lambda}_{k}+\zeta_{\bm{\lambda},k}\widehat{\nabla}_{\bm{\lambda}}\mathcal{L}_{\omega}(\bm{\upsilon}_{k+1},\bm{\lambda}_{k})\right),

where
\zeta_{\bm{\upsilon},k},\zeta_{\bm{\lambda},k}>0
are the learning rates and
\widehat{\nabla}_{\bm{\upsilon}}\mathcal{L}_{\omega}(\bm{\upsilon}_{k},\bm{\lambda}_{k}),\widehat{\nabla}_{\bm{\lambda}}\mathcal{L}_{\omega}(\bm{\upsilon}_{k},\bm{\lambda}_{k})
are (unbiased) estimators of the gradients
{\nabla}_{\bm{\upsilon}}\mathcal{L}_{\omega}(\bm{\upsilon}_{k},\bm{\lambda}_{k}),{\nabla}_{\bm{\lambda}}\mathcal{L}_{\omega}(\bm{\upsilon}_{k},\bm{\lambda}_{k})
of the regularized Lagrangian function. Notice that
C-PG
performs
alternate
descent-ascent, as the update value for the dual variable is performed employing the
already updated
primal variable.

3.2
Assumptions

Before diving into the study of the convergence guarantees of
C-PG
, we list and motivate the assumptions necessary for our analysis.

Assumption 3.1

(Existence of Saddle Points)
.

There exist
\bm{\upsilon}^{*}_{0}\in\mathcal{V}
and
\bm{\lambda}^{*}_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{U}_{\geq 0}
such that
\mathcal{L}_{0}(\bm{\upsilon}^{*}_{0},\bm{\lambda}^{*}_{0})=\min_{\bm{\upsilon}\in\mathcal{V}}\max_{\bm{\lambda}{\in\mathbb{R}^{U}_{\geq 0}}}\mathcal{L}_{0}(\bm{\upsilon},\bm{\lambda})
.

Assumption
3.1
ensures that the value of the min-max problem is attained by a pair of primal-dual values
\bm{\upsilon}^{*}_{0}\in\mathcal{V}
and
\bm{\lambda}^{*}_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{U}_{\geq 0}
which, consequently, satisfy
\mathcal{L}_{0}(\bm{\upsilon}^{*}_{0},\bm{\lambda})\leq\mathcal{L}_{0}(\bm{\upsilon}^{*}_{0},\bm{\lambda}^{*}_{0})\leq\mathcal{L}_{0}(\bm{\upsilon},\bm{\lambda}^{*}_{0})
for every
\bm{\upsilon}\in\mathcal{V}
and
\bm{\lambda}\in\mathbb{R}^{U}_{\geq 0}
. Analogous assumptions have been considered by
Yang et al. 2020
and
Ying et al. 2022
. Thus,
(\bm{\upsilon}^{*}_{0},\bm{\lambda}^{*}_{0})
is a saddle point of the Lagrangian function
\mathcal{L}_{0}
and, consequently,
strong duality
holds. Alternatively, as commonly requested in CRL works, assuming
Slater’s condition
combined with the requirement that the policy space covers all Markovian policies ensures strong duality
(
Paternain et al. 2019
;
Ding et al. 2020
;
Ding et al. 2024
, e.g.,)
.
4
4

4

Assumption
3.1
combined with Slater’s condition, i.e., the existence of a parametrization
\widetilde{\bm{\upsilon}}\in\mathcal{V}
for which there exists
\xi>0
such that
J_{i}(\widetilde{\bm{\upsilon}})-b<-\xi
for all
i\in\mathopen{[\![}U\mathclose{]\!]}
(strictly feasible), allows providing an upper bound to the Lagrange multipliers
\|\bm{\lambda}^{*}_{0}\|_{2}\leq\xi^{-1}(J_{0}(\widetilde{\bm{\upsilon}})-J_{0}(\bm{\upsilon}^{*}_{0}))
using standard arguments
(
Ying et al. 2022
, see)
.

Assumption 3.2

(Weak
\psi
-Gradient Domination)
.

Let
\psi\in[1,2]
. There exist
\alpha_{1}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
and
\beta_{1}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
such that, for every
\bm{\upsilon}\in\mathcal{V}
and
\bm{\lambda}{\in\Lambda}
, it holds that:

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla_{\bm{\upsilon}}\mathcal{L}_{0}(\bm{\upsilon},\bm{\lambda})\right\|_{2}^{\psi}\geq\alpha_{1}\Big(\mathcal{L}_{0}(\bm{\upsilon},\bm{\lambda})-\min_{\bm{\upsilon}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}}\mathcal{L}_{0}(\bm{\upsilon}^{\prime},\bm{\lambda})\Big)-\beta_{1}.

(2)

Assumption
3.2
is customary in the convergence analysis of policy gradient methods and it is usually enforced on the objective
J_{0}
only
(
Yuan et al. 2022
;
Masiha et al. 2022
;
Fatkhullin et al. 2023
)
. In particular, when
\beta_{1}=0
, we speak of strong
\psi
-gradient domination. In this form, for a generic exponent
\psi\in[1,2]
, this assumption has been employed by
Masiha et al. 2022
. Particular cases are
\psi=1
, which corresponds to the standard weak
gradient domination
(GD), while for
\psi=2
we have the so-called
Polyak-Łojasiewicz
(PL) condition. Notice that Assumption
3.2
is enforced on the non-regularized Lagrangian function
\mathcal{L}_{0}
(i.e.,
\omega=0
). However, it is easy to realize that it holds for the regularized one
\mathcal{L}_{\omega}
by simply replacing
\mathcal{L}_{0}
with
\mathcal{L}_{\omega}
in Equation (
2
).

Remark 3.1

(When does Assumption
3.2
holds?)
.

As remarked by
Ding et al. 2024
, the Lagrangian function, for a fixed value of
\bm{\lambda}
can be regarded as the return of a new reward function
-C_{0}-\langle\bm{\lambda},\mathbf{C}\rangle
, where
\mathbf{C}=(C_{1},\dots,C_{U})^{\top}
. As a consequence, a sufficient condition for Assumption
3.2
is when the selected class of policies guarantees the
\psi
-gradient domination
regardless
of the reward function. For instance, in tabular environments with natural policy parametrization, i.e.,
\pi_{\bm{\theta}}(s)=\bm{\theta}_{s}
for every
s\in\mathcal{S}
, the PL condition (
\psi=2
and
\beta_{1}=0
) holds
(
Bhandari and Russo 2024
)
. Moreover, in tabular environments with softmax policy, i.e.,
\pi_{\bm{\theta}}(a|s)\propto\exp(\theta(s,a))
, GD (
\psi=1
and
\beta_{1}=0
) holds
(
Mei et al. 2020
)
. This enables a meaningful comparison of our results when resorting to softmax policies
(
Ding et al. 2020
;
Gladin et al. 2023
;
Ding et al. 2024
, e.g.,)
. More in general, when (
i
) the Fisher information matrix induced by policy
\pi_{\bm{\theta}}
is non-degenerate for every
\bm{\theta}\in\Theta
, i.e.,
\mathbf{F}(\bm{\theta})=\E_{\pi_{\bm{\theta}}}[\nabla_{\bm{\theta}}\log\pi_{\bm{\theta}}(\bm{a}|\bm{s})\nabla_{\bm{\theta}}\log\pi_{\bm{\theta}}(\bm{a}|\bm{s})^{\top}]\succeq\mu_{\text{F}}\mathbf{I}
for some
\mu_{\text{F}}>0

and (
ii
) a compatible function approximation bias bound holds, i.e.,
\E_{\pi_{\bm{\theta}^{*}}}[(A^{\pi_{\bm{\theta}}}(\bm{s},\bm{a})-(1-\gamma)\bm{u}^{\top}\nabla_{\bm{\theta}}\log\pi_{\bm{\theta}}(\bm{a}|\bm{s}))^{2}]\leq\epsilon_{\text{bias}}
being
\bm{u}=\mathbf{F}(\bm{\theta})^{\dagger}\nabla_{\bm{\theta}}J_{0}(\bm{\theta})
and the advantage function
A^{\pi_{\bm{\theta}}}
computed w.r.t. reward
-c_{0}-\langle\bm{\lambda},\mathbf{c}\rangle
, the weak GD (
\psi=1
) holds with
\alpha_{1}=G\mu^{-1}_{F}
and
\beta_{1}=(1-\gamma)^{-1}\sqrt{\epsilon_{\text{bias}}}
, where
G
is such that
\|\nabla_{\bm{\theta}}\log\pi_{\bm{\theta}}(\bm{a}|\bm{s})\|_{2}\leq G

(
Masiha et al. 2022
)
.

In principle, we could have enforced Assumption
3.2
on the primal function
H_{\omega}(\bm{\upsilon})
only. However, this would come with two drawbacks: (
i
) the assumption would now depend explicitly on
\omega
; (
ii
) the considerations of Remark
3.1
would no longer hold. Nevertheless, in Lemma
A.4
, we prove that Assumption
3.2
induces an analogous property on the primal function
H_{\omega}(\bm{\upsilon})
in the regularized case.

Assumption 3.3

(Regularity of the Regularized Lagrangian
\mathcal{L}_{0}
)
.

There exist
L_{1},L_{2},L_{3}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
such that, for every
\bm{\upsilon},\bm{\upsilon}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}
, and for every
\bm{\lambda},\bm{\lambda}^{\prime}{\in\Lambda}
, the following holds:

\nabla_{\bm{\lambda}}\mathcal{L}_{0}(\cdot,\bm{\lambda})

L_{1}
-Lipschitz w.r.t.
\bm{\upsilon}
:

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla_{\bm{\lambda}}\mathcal{L}_{0}(\bm{\upsilon},\bm{\lambda})-\nabla_{\bm{\lambda}}\mathcal{L}_{0}(\bm{\upsilon}^{\prime},\bm{\lambda})\right\|_{2}\leq L_{1}\!\left\|\bm{\upsilon}-\bm{\upsilon}^{\prime}\right\|_{2},

(3)

\displaystyle\text{$\mathcal{L}_{0}(\cdot,\bm{\lambda})$ $L_{2}$-Smooth w.r.t. $\bm{\upsilon}$}:

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla_{\bm{\upsilon}}\mathcal{L}_{0}(\bm{\upsilon},\bm{\lambda})-\nabla_{\bm{\upsilon}}\mathcal{L}_{0}(\bm{\upsilon}^{\prime},\bm{\lambda})\right\|_{2}\leq L_{2}\|\bm{\upsilon}-\bm{\upsilon}^{\prime}\|_{2},

(4)

\nabla_{\bm{\upsilon}}\mathcal{L}_{0}(\bm{\upsilon},\cdot)

L_{3}
-Lipschitz w.r.t
\bm{\lambda}
:

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla_{\bm{\upsilon}}\mathcal{L}_{0}(\bm{\upsilon},\bm{\lambda})-\nabla_{\bm{\upsilon}}\mathcal{L}_{0}(\bm{\upsilon},\bm{\lambda}^{\prime})\right\|_{2}\leq L_{3}\|\bm{\lambda}-\bm{\lambda}^{\prime}\|_{2}.

(5)

Notice that, similarly to Assumption
3.2
, we realize that if Assumption
3.3
holds for the non-regularized Lagrangian
\mathcal{L}_{0}
, it also holds (with the same constants) for the regularized one
\mathcal{L}_{\omega}
for every
\omega>0
. The regularity conditions of Assumption
3.3
are common in the literature
(
Yang et al. 2020
)
and mild when regarded from the policy optimization perspective.
Equation (
3
) is satisfied whenever the constraint functions
J_{i}
are Lipschitz continuous w.r.t.
\bm{\upsilon}
. Indeed,
\left\|\nabla_{\bm{\lambda}}\mathcal{L}_{0}(\bm{\upsilon},\bm{\lambda})-\nabla_{\bm{\lambda}}\mathcal{L}_{0}(\bm{\upsilon}^{\prime},\bm{\lambda})\right\|_{2}=\left\|\mathbf{J}(\bm{\upsilon})-\mathbf{J}(\bm{\upsilon}^{\prime})\right\|_{2}
. Equation (
4
) is fulfilled when the objective function
J_{0}
and the constraint functions
J_{i}
are smooth w.r.t.
\bm{\upsilon}
and the Lagrange multipliers are bounded (guaranteed thanks to the projection
\Pi_{\Lambda}
), since
\left\|\nabla_{\bm{\upsilon}}\mathcal{L}_{0}(\bm{\upsilon},\bm{\lambda})-\nabla_{\bm{\upsilon}}\mathcal{L}_{0}(\bm{\upsilon}^{\prime},\bm{\lambda})\right\|_{2}\leq|\nabla_{\bm{\upsilon}}J_{0}(\bm{\upsilon})-\nabla_{\bm{\upsilon}}J_{0}(\bm{\upsilon}^{\prime})|+\sum_{i=1}^{U}\lambda_{i}|\nabla_{\bm{\upsilon}}J_{i}(\bm{\upsilon})-\nabla_{\bm{\upsilon}}J_{i}(\bm{\upsilon})|
. Finally, Equation (
5
) is fulfilled whenever functions
J_{i}
admit bounded gradients, since
\left\|\nabla_{\bm{\upsilon}}\mathcal{L}_{0}(\bm{\upsilon},\bm{\lambda})-\nabla_{\bm{\upsilon}}\mathcal{L}_{0}(\bm{\upsilon},\bm{\lambda}^{\prime})\right\|_{2}\leq\|\nabla_{\bm{\upsilon}}\mathbf{J}(\bm{\upsilon})(\bm{\lambda}-\bm{\lambda}^{\prime})\|_{2}
.
It is worth noting that
L_{2}
depends on the norm of the Lagrange multipliers and, consequently, due to the projection operator
\Pi_{\Lambda}
, we have that
L_{2}=\mathcal{O}(\omega^{-1})
, whereas
L_{1}
and
L_{3}
are independent on
\omega
.
5
5

5

We highlight the dependencies on
\omega
since, as we shall see later, we will set
\omega=\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)
having, consequently, an effect on the convergence rate.

Explicit conditions on the constitutive elements of the MDP and (hyper)policies to ensure Lipshitzness and smoothness of these quantities are reported in
(
Montenegro et al. 2024a
, Appendix E)
for both the
AB
and
PB
cases.
These regularity properties enforced on
\mathcal{L}_{\omega}
are inherited by the primal function
H_{\omega}
which results to be
\left(L_{2}+L_{1}^{2}\omega^{-1}\right)
-LS (Lemma
A.7
).
Concerning the regularity of
\mathcal{L}_{\omega}
w.r.t.
\bm{\lambda}
, we observe that it is a quadratic function and, therefore, it is
\omega
-smooth and satisfies the PL condition, i.e., Assumption
3.2
with
\psi=2
,
\beta_{1}=0
, and with
\alpha_{1}=\omega
(Lemma
A.5
).

Assumption 3.4

(Bounded Estimator Variance)
.

For every
\bm{\upsilon}\in\mathcal{V}
and
\bm{\lambda}\in\Lambda
, the estimators
\widehat{\nabla}_{\bm{\upsilon}}\mathcal{L}_{\omega}(\bm{\upsilon},\bm{\lambda})
and
\widehat{\nabla}_{\bm{\lambda}}\mathcal{L}_{\omega}(\bm{\upsilon},\bm{\lambda})
are unbiased for
{\nabla}_{\bm{\upsilon}}\mathcal{L}_{\omega}(\bm{\upsilon},\bm{\lambda})=\nabla_{\bm{\upsilon}}J_{0}(\bm{\upsilon})+\sum_{i=1}^{U}\lambda_{i}\nabla_{\bm{\upsilon}}J_{i}(\bm{\upsilon})
and
{\nabla}_{\bm{\lambda}}\mathcal{L}_{\omega}(\bm{\upsilon},\bm{\lambda})=\mathbf{J}(\bm{\upsilon})-\mathbf{b}-\omega\bm{\lambda}
with bounded variance, i.e., there exist
V_{\bm{\upsilon}},V_{\bm{\lambda}}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
such that:

\displaystyle\Var[\widehat{\nabla}_{\bm{\upsilon}}\mathcal{L}_{\omega}(\bm{\upsilon},\bm{\lambda})]\leq V_{\bm{\upsilon}},\qquad\Var[\widehat{\nabla}_{\bm{\lambda}}\mathcal{L}_{\omega}(\bm{\upsilon},\bm{\lambda})]\leq V_{\bm{\lambda}}.

Note that
V_{\bm{\upsilon}}
typically depends on the Lagrange multipliers and, for standard sample-mean estimators, it is of order
V_{\bm{\upsilon}}=\mathcal{O}(\omega^{-2})
thanks to the projection operator. In contrast,
V_{\bm{\lambda}}
is usually not affected by
\omega
since the term
\omega\bm{\lambda}
is not estimated and, thus, it does not affect the variance of the sample mean estimator.
The variance of such estimators can be easily controlled by leveraging on the properties of the score function as done in previous works (see
Papini et al. 2022
and
Montenegro et al. 2024a
, Appendix E).

3.3
Convergence Analysis

We are now ready to tackle the convergence analysis of
C-PG
to the global optimum of the COP of Equation (
1
). To this end, we study the
potential function
defined as
\mathcal{P}_{k}(\chi)\coloneqq a_{k}+\chi b_{k}
, where
a_{k}\coloneqq\E[H_{\omega}(\bm{\upsilon}_{k})-H^{*}_{\omega}]
and
b_{k}\coloneqq\E[H_{\omega}(\bm{\upsilon}_{k})-\mathcal{L}_{\omega}(\bm{\upsilon}_{k},\bm{\lambda}_{k})]
,
\chi\in(0,1)
will be specified later, and the expectation is taken w.r.t. the stochastic process generating samples. Since
a_{k},b_{k}\geq 0
, intuitively, if
\mathcal{P}_{k}(\chi)\approx 0
we have that both
a_{k},b_{k}\approx 0
and, consequently, convergence is achieved.
Let us start relating
\mathcal{P}_{k}(\chi)
, with the solution of the COP in Equation (
1
).

Theorem 3.1

(Objective Function Gap and Constraint Violation)
.

Let
\epsilon\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
. Under Assumption
3.1
, if
\mathcal{P}_{k}(\chi)\leq\epsilon
, it holds that:

\displaystyle\E[J_{0}(\bm{\upsilon}_{k})-J_{0}(\bm{\upsilon}^{*}_{0})]\leq\epsilon+\frac{\omega}{2}\|\bm{\lambda}^{*}_{0}\|_{2}^{2},\qquad\E[(J_{i}(\bm{\upsilon}_{k})-b_{i})^{+}]\leq 4\epsilon+\omega\|\bm{\lambda}^{*}_{0}\|_{2},\quad\forall i\in\mathopen{[\![}U\mathclose{]\!]}.

(6)

Proof.

Since
\mathcal{P}_{k}(\chi)\leq\epsilon
, it follows that
a_{k}\leq\epsilon
and, consequently,
0\leq\E[H_{\omega}(\bm{\upsilon}_{k})-H^{*}_{\omega}]\leq\epsilon
. We start by bounding the norm of the dual variables:

\displaystyle\|\bm{\lambda}^{*}(\bm{\upsilon}_{k})\|_{2}\leq\|\bm{\lambda}^{*}_{\omega}\|_{2}+\|\bm{\lambda}^{*}(\bm{\upsilon}_{k})-\bm{\lambda}^{*}_{\omega}\|_{2}\leq\|\bm{\lambda}^{*}_{\omega}\|_{2}+\frac{4}{\omega}(H_{\omega}(\bm{\upsilon}_{k})-H^{*}_{\omega}),

where we applied the triangular inequality and Lemma
A.6
, which proves that, for any
\bm{\upsilon}\in\mathcal{V}
,
H_{\omega}(\bm{\upsilon})-H_{\omega}^{*}\geq\frac{\omega}{4}\|\bm{\lambda}^{*}(\bm{\upsilon})-\bm{\lambda}^{*}_{\omega}\|_{2}
.
The projection
\Pi_{\Lambda}
is such that
\bm{\lambda}^{*}(\bm{\upsilon})=\Pi_{\Lambda}\left(\frac{1}{\omega}(\mathbf{J}(\bm{\upsilon})-\mathbf{b})\right)=\frac{1}{\omega}(\mathbf{J}(\bm{\upsilon})-\mathbf{b})^{+}
and, consequently, we have:

\displaystyle\|(\mathbf{J}(\bm{\upsilon}_{k})-\mathbf{b})^{+}\|_{2}-\|(\mathbf{J}(\bm{\upsilon}^{*}_{\omega})-\mathbf{b})^{+}\|_{2}\leq 4(H_{\omega}(\bm{\upsilon}_{k})-H^{*}_{\omega}).

By the last inequality, together with Lemma
A.3
, which states that:

\displaystyle 0\leq J_{0}(\bm{\upsilon}^{*}_{0})-J_{0}(\bm{\upsilon}^{*}_{\omega})\leq\omega\|\bm{\lambda}^{*}_{0}\|_{2}^{2}\quad\text{and}\quad\|(\mathbf{J}(\bm{\upsilon}^{*}_{\omega})-\mathbf{b})^{+}\|_{2}\leq\omega\|\bm{\lambda}^{*}_{0}\|_{2},

and applying the expectation on both sides, we have the following:

\displaystyle\E[\|(\mathbf{J}(\bm{\upsilon}_{k})-\mathbf{b})^{+}\|_{2}]\leq\|(\mathbf{J}(\bm{\upsilon}^{*}_{\omega})-\mathbf{b})^{+}\|_{2}+4\E[H_{\omega}(\bm{\upsilon}_{k})-H^{*}_{\omega}]\leq\omega\|\bm{\lambda}^{*}_{0}\|_{2}+{4\epsilon}.

We obtain the constraint violation bound recalling that:

\displaystyle\E[\|(\mathbf{J}(\bm{\upsilon}_{k})-\mathbf{b}))^{+}\|_{2}]\geq\left\|\E[(\mathbf{J}(\bm{\upsilon}_{k})-\mathbf{b})^{+}]\right\|_{2}\geq\|\E[(\mathbf{J}(\bm{\upsilon}_{k})-\mathbf{b})^{+}]\|_{\infty}.

For the objective function bound, let us consider the following derivation. By definition of
H_{\omega}(\bm{\upsilon})
and
\bm{\lambda}^{*}(\bm{\upsilon})
we have:

\displaystyle J_{0}(\bm{\upsilon}_{k})-J_{0}(\bm{\upsilon}^{*}_{\omega})

\displaystyle=H_{\omega}(\bm{\upsilon}_{k})-H^{*}_{\omega}-\frac{\omega}{2}\left(\|\bm{\lambda}^{*}(\bm{\upsilon}_{k})\|_{2}^{2}-\|\bm{\lambda}^{*}_{\omega}\|_{2}^{2}\right).

Taking the expectation on both sides and upper bounding
\|\bm{\lambda}^{*}_{\omega}\|
with
\|\bm{\lambda}^{*}_{0}\|
from Lemma
A.1
, which states that
0\leq\mathcal{L}_{0}(\bm{\upsilon}^{*}_{0},\bm{\lambda}^{*}_{0})-\mathcal{L}_{0}(\bm{\upsilon}^{*}_{\omega},\bm{\lambda}^{*}_{\omega})\leq\frac{\omega}{2}\left(\|\bm{\lambda}^{*}_{0}\|_{2}^{2}-\|\bm{\lambda}^{*}_{\omega}\|_{2}^{2}\right)
, the following holds:

\displaystyle\E[J_{0}(\bm{\upsilon}_{k})-J_{0}(\bm{\upsilon}^{*}_{\omega})]

\displaystyle=\E[H_{\omega}(\bm{\upsilon}_{k})-H^{*}_{\omega}]-\frac{\omega}{2}\E[\|\bm{\lambda}^{*}(\bm{\upsilon}_{k})\|_{2}^{2}-\|\bm{\lambda}^{*}_{\omega}\|_{2}^{2}]

\displaystyle\leq\E[H_{\omega}(\bm{\upsilon}_{k})-H^{*}_{\omega}]+\frac{\omega}{2}\|\bm{\lambda}^{*}_{\omega}\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon+\frac{\omega}{2}\|\bm{\lambda}^{*}_{0}\|_{2}^{2}.

The result is obtained by applying Lemma
A.3
(already stated in this proof) as follows:

\displaystyle\E[J_{0}(\bm{\upsilon}_{k})-J_{0}(\bm{\upsilon}^{*}_{0})]=\E[J_{0}(\bm{\upsilon}_{k})-J_{0}(\bm{\upsilon}^{*}_{\omega})]+\underbrace{J_{0}(\bm{\upsilon}^{*}_{\omega})-J_{0}(\bm{\upsilon}^{*}_{0})}_{\leq 0}.

∎

Theorem
3.1
justifies the study of the potential
\mathcal{P}_{k}(\chi)
as a technical tool to ensure convergence. Indeed, whenever
\mathcal{P}_{k}(\chi)\leq\epsilon
both (
i
)
the objective function gap and (
ii
) the constraint violation scale linearly with
\epsilon
and with the regularization parameter
\omega
of the regularized Lagrangian
\mathcal{L}_{\omega}
multiplied by the norm of the Lagrange multipliers of the non-regularized problem
\|\bm{\lambda}^{*}_{0}\|_{2}
, which are finite under Assumption
3.1
. This expression also suggests a choice of
\omega=\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)
to enforce an overall
\epsilon
error on both quantities. Note that, from Theorem
3.1
, it is immediate to employ a
conservative constraint
(
b_{i}^{\prime}\approx b_{i}-4\epsilon-\omega\|\bm{\lambda}^{*}_{0}\|_{2}
) to achieve zero constraint violation with no modification of the algorithm.

We are now ready to state the convergence guarantees for the potential function.

Theorem 3.2

(Convergence of
\mathcal{P}_{K}
)
.

Under Assumptions
3.2
,
3.3
,
3.4
, for
\chi<1/5
, sufficiently small
\epsilon
and
\omega
, and a choice of
constant
learning rates
\zeta_{\bm{\upsilon}},\zeta_{\bm{\lambda}}
, we have
\mathcal{P}_{K}(\chi)\leq\epsilon+\beta_{1}/\alpha_{1}
whenever:
6
6

6

In the context of this statement, the
\mathcal{O}(\cdot)
notation preserves dependences on
\epsilon
and
\omega
only.

•

K=\mathcal{O}(\omega^{-1}\log(\epsilon^{-1}))
if
\psi=2
and the gradients are exact (i.e.,
V_{\bm{\upsilon}}=V_{\bm{\lambda}}=0
);

•

K=\mathcal{O}(\omega^{-1}\epsilon^{-\frac{2}{\psi}-1})
if
\psi\in[1,2)
and the gradients are exact (i.e.,
V_{\bm{\upsilon}}=V_{\bm{\lambda}}=0
);

•

K=\mathcal{O}(\omega^{-3}\epsilon^{-\frac{4}{\psi}+1})
if
\psi\in[1,2]
and the gradients are estimated (i.e.,
V_{\bm{\upsilon}}=\mathcal{O}(\omega^{-2})
and
V_{\bm{\lambda}}=\mathcal{O}(1)
).

Proof Sketch.

The proof of Theorem
3.2
is quite technical, thus we report here just its sketch, which we divide into five parts.

Part I: bounding
a_{k}
.
The
first part
of the proof consists of bounding
\E[a_{k+1}\mid\mathcal{F}_{k-1}]=\E[H_{\omega}(\bm{\upsilon}_{k+1})-H^{*}\mid\mathcal{F}_{k-1}]
, considering to be at a generic
k
th
iterate of
C-PG
with
\mathcal{F}_{k-1}
a filtration up to iteration
k-1
. In particular, by exploiting the update rule of
C-PG
, via Lemma
A.7
stating that
H_{\omega}
is
L_{H}
-LS, and by selecting
\zeta_{\bm{\upsilon},k}\leq L_{H}
, we can conclude that:

\displaystyle\E\left[H_{\omega}(\bm{\upsilon}_{k+1})|\mathcal{F}_{k-1}\right]-H^{*}

\displaystyle\leq H_{\omega}(\bm{\upsilon}_{k})-H^{*}-\frac{\zeta_{\bm{\upsilon},k}}{2}\left\|\nabla_{\bm{\upsilon}}H_{\omega}(\bm{\upsilon}_{k})\right\|_{2}^{2}+\frac{\zeta_{\bm{\upsilon},k}}{2}\left\|\nabla_{\bm{\upsilon}}\mathcal{L}_{\omega}(\bm{\upsilon}_{k},\bm{\lambda}_{k})-\nabla_{\bm{\upsilon}}H_{\omega}(\bm{\upsilon}_{k})\right\|_{2}^{2}+\frac{L_{H}}{2}\zeta_{\bm{\upsilon},k}^{2}V_{\bm{\upsilon}},

where the constant
V_{\bm{\upsilon}}
, coming from Assumption
3.4
, is such that
\Var[\nabla_{\bm{\upsilon}}\mathcal{L}_{\omega}(\bm{\upsilon}_{k},\bm{\lambda}_{k})]\leq V_{\bm{\upsilon}}
.

Part II: bounding
b_{k}
.
Similarly to what shown in the first part, the
second part
consists of bounding
\E[b_{k+1}\mid\mathcal{F}_{k-1}]=\E[H_{\omega}(\bm{\upsilon}_{k-1})-\mathcal{L}_{\omega}(\bm{\upsilon}_{k+1},\bm{\lambda}_{k+1})\mid\mathcal{F}_{k-1}]
. To do so, we exploit Assumption
3.3
stating that
\mathcal{L}_{\omega}
is
L_{2}
-LS. In particular, in Lemma
A.5
, we show that
\mathcal{L}_{\omega}
is
\omega
-LS and that it fulfills the PL condition with constant
\omega
. From these observations, together with the update rule of
C-PG
and the selection
\zeta_{\bm{\lambda},k}\leq 1/\omega
, we conclude that:

\displaystyle\E\left[H_{\omega}(\bm{\upsilon}_{k+1})-\mathcal{L}_{\omega}(\bm{\upsilon}_{k+1},\bm{\lambda}_{k+1})|\mathcal{F}_{k-1}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\left(1-\frac{\zeta_{\bm{\lambda},k}}{2}\omega\right)\left(H_{\omega}(\bm{\upsilon}_{k})-\mathcal{L}_{\omega}(\bm{\upsilon}_{k},\bm{\lambda}_{k})\right)+\left(1-\frac{\zeta_{\bm{\lambda},k}}{2}\omega\right)\left(\zeta_{\bm{\upsilon},k}\left(1+\frac{L_{2}}{2}\zeta_{\bm{\upsilon},k}\right)\left\|\nabla_{\bm{\upsilon}}\mathcal{L}_{\omega}(\bm{\upsilon}_{k},\bm{\lambda}_{k})\right\|_{2}^{2}+\frac{L_{2}}{2}\zeta_{\bm{\upsilon},k}^{2}V_{\bm{\upsilon}}\right)

\displaystyle\quad+\left(1-\frac{\zeta_{\bm{\lambda},k}}{2}\omega\right)\left(-\frac{\zeta_{\bm{\upsilon},k}}{2}\left\|\nabla_{\bm{\upsilon}}H_{\omega}(\bm{\upsilon}_{k})\right\|_{2}^{2}+\frac{\zeta_{\bm{\upsilon},k}}{2}\left\|\nabla_{\bm{\upsilon}}\mathcal{L}_{\omega}(\bm{\upsilon}_{k},\bm{\lambda}_{k})-\nabla_{\bm{\upsilon}}H_{\omega}(\bm{\upsilon}_{k})\right\|_{2}^{2}+\frac{L_{H}}{2}\zeta_{\bm{\upsilon},k}^{2}V_{\bm{\upsilon}}\right)+\frac{\omega}{2}\zeta_{\bm{\lambda},k}^{2}V_{\bm{\lambda}}.

Part III: bounding
\mathcal{P}_{k}(\chi)
.
Having bounded separately
a_{k}
and
b_{k}
, and being
\mathcal{P}_{k}(\chi)=a_{k}+\chi b_{k}
, we can just put together the previously obtained results to have a bound on
\mathcal{P}_{k}(\chi)
. Moreover, exploiting Assumption
3.3
and by noticing that
\mathcal{L}_{\omega}
satisfies the quadratic growth condition (since Lemma
A.5
states that
\mathcal{L}_{\omega}
satisfies the PL condition with
\omega
as constant), we obtain the following inequality:

\displaystyle a_{k+1}+\chi b_{k+1}

\displaystyle\leq a_{k}+\chi\left(1-\frac{\zeta_{\bm{\lambda},k}}{2}\omega\right)b_{k}

\displaystyle\quad+\left(2\zeta_{\bm{\upsilon},k}\left(1+\frac{L_{2}}{2}\zeta_{\bm{\upsilon},k}\right)\chi\left(1-\frac{\zeta_{\bm{\lambda},k}}{2}\omega\right)\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\left.-\frac{\zeta_{\bm{\upsilon},k}}{2}\left(1+\chi\left(1-\frac{\zeta_{\bm{\lambda},k}}{2}\omega\right)\right)\right)\E\left[\left\|\nabla_{\bm{\upsilon}}H_{\omega}(\bm{\upsilon}_{k})\right\|_{2}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\quad+\left(2\zeta_{\bm{\upsilon},k}\left(1+\frac{L_{2}}{2}\zeta_{\bm{\upsilon},k}\right)\chi\left(1-\frac{\zeta_{\bm{\lambda},k}}{2}\omega\right)+\frac{\zeta_{\bm{\upsilon},k}}{2}\left(1+\chi\left(1-\frac{\zeta_{\bm{\lambda},k}}{2}\omega\right)\right)\right)\frac{4L_{3}^{2}}{\omega}b_{k}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{\zeta_{\bm{\upsilon},k}^{2}}{2}\left(L_{H}+\chi\left(1-\frac{\zeta_{\bm{\lambda},k}}{2}\omega\right)(L_{H}+L_{2})\right)V_{\bm{\upsilon}}+\chi\frac{\omega}{2}\zeta_{\bm{\lambda},k}^{2}V_{\bm{\lambda}}.

Part IV: applying the
\psi
-gradient domination.
Form the previously highlighted inequality, we aim at recovering a recursive equation in
\mathcal{P}_{k}(\chi)
. To this end, we apply Assumption
3.2
, from which it follows that

\displaystyle\E\left[\left\|\nabla_{\bm{\upsilon}}H_{\omega}(\bm{\upsilon})\right\|_{2}^{2}\right]\geq\alpha_{1}^{\frac{2}{\psi}}

\displaystyle\max\left\{0,\;\E\left[H_{\omega}(\bm{\upsilon})-\widetilde{H}^{*}\right]\right\}^{\frac{2}{\psi}},

where
\widetilde{H}^{*}\coloneqq H^{*}+\beta_{1}/\alpha_{1}
. Now, exploiting this last result and enforcing
\chi\leq\min\{1/5,\;1/(\max_{k\in\mathopen{[\![}K\mathclose{]\!]}}b_{k})\}
, after many algebraic steps, we obtain the following inequality:

\displaystyle\widetilde{P}_{k+1}(\chi)\leq\widetilde{P}_{k}(\chi)-\widetilde{C}\max\left\{0,\;\widetilde{P}_{k}(\chi)\right\}^{\frac{2}{\psi}}+\widetilde{V},

where
\widetilde{P}_{k}(\chi)\coloneqq a_{k}+\chi b_{k}-\beta_{1}/\alpha_{1}
,
\widetilde{C}\coloneqq 2^{1-\frac{1}{\psi}}\frac{\zeta_{\bm{\upsilon},k}\alpha_{1}^{\frac{2}{\psi}}}{2}
, and
\widetilde{V}\coloneqq\frac{\zeta_{\bm{\upsilon},k}^{2}}{2}\left((1+2\chi)L_{2}+{(1+\chi)}\frac{L_{1}^{2}}{\omega}\right)V_{\bm{\upsilon}}+\chi\frac{\omega}{2}\zeta_{\bm{\lambda},k}^{2}V_{\bm{\lambda}}
. We highlight that to get to this result, the learning rates have been selected as:

\displaystyle\zeta_{\bm{\upsilon},k}\leq\min\left\{\frac{1}{L_{H}},\frac{1}{L_{2}},\frac{\omega^{2}\chi\zeta_{\bm{\lambda},k}}{(1+\chi)\omega\alpha_{1}^{\frac{2}{\psi}}+4L^{2}_{3}(1+7\chi)}\right\}\quad\text{and}\quad\zeta_{\bm{\lambda},k}\leq\frac{1}{\omega}.

Part V: rates computation.
Equipped with the recursive inequality reported in Part IV, we just have to compute the rates guaranteeing
\mathcal{P}_{K}(\chi)\leq\epsilon+\beta_{1}/\alpha_{1}
. In particular, we first analyze the
exact gradient
case, i.e.,
\widetilde{V}=0
, for when
\psi=2
, and
\psi\in[1,2)
. Then, we do the same in the case of
estimated gradients
, i.e.,
\widetilde{V}>0
. All the results are reported in Table
1
.

Some comments are in order. First, Theorem
3.2
holds for a specific choice of the constant
\chi\in(0,1/5)
defining the potential function
\mathcal{P}_{K}(\chi)
. Second, the presented rates hold for sufficiently small values of
\epsilon
and
\omega
. This is just for presentation purposes, as the sample complexity
7
7

7

Theorem
3.2
provides an
iteration-complexity
guarantee. Concerning the
estimated gradient
case, this translates into a
sample complexity
guarantee since we are allowed to estimate gradients with a single sample.
can only improve if we increase the values of
\epsilon
and
\omega
. Third, in the proof, an explicit expression of the learning rates is provided. Concerning their orders, for the case of exact gradients, we choose
\zeta_{\bm{\lambda}}=\omega^{-1}
and
\zeta_{\bm{\upsilon}}=\mathcal{O}(\omega)
, whereas for the estimated gradient case, we choose
\zeta_{\bm{\lambda}}=\mathcal{O}(\omega\epsilon^{2/\psi})
and
\zeta_{\bm{\upsilon}}=\mathcal{O}(\omega^{3}\epsilon^{2/\psi})
.

Assuming
\omega
to be a constant, we observe that both learning rates display the same dependence on
\epsilon
and, consequently, they are in
single-time scale
. However, as we have seen in Theorem
3.1
, in order to obtain guarantees on the original non-regularized problem, we have to set
\omega=\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)
, leading to a
two-time scales
algorithm. Fourth, we observe that, for both exact and estimated gradients, the sample complexity degrades as the constant
\psi
of the gradient domination moves from
2
to
1
, delivering the smallest sample complexity when the PL condition holds.
Finally, we highlight that
C-PG
jointly: (
i
) converges to the global optimum of the COP problem of Equation (
1
); (
ii
) delivers a
last-iterate
guarantee; (
iii
) has no dependence on the cardinality of the state or action spaces, making it completely
dimension-free
.

.

3.4
Action-based
and
Parameter-based
Variants of
C-PG

So far, we have focused on the exploration-agnostic formulation of the proposed method. In this section, we introduce its action-based and parameter-based variants, namely
C-PGAE
and
C-PGPE
.
These variants differ in the considered cost functions,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.4648,0.7344}J_{\text{A},i}}
or
{\color[rgb]{0.8008,0.1992,0.0664}J_{\text{P},i}}
for every
i\in\mathopen{[\![}0,U\mathclose{]\!]}
, and the estimators employed to update the optimization variables.
We recall that the general Lagrangian function for the problem in Equation (
1
) is the following:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\dagger,\omega}(\bm{\upsilon},\bm{\lambda})\coloneqq J_{\dagger,0}(\bm{\upsilon})+\sum_{u=1}^{U}\lambda_{u}\left(J_{\dagger,u}(\bm{\upsilon})-b_{u}\right)-\frac{\omega}{2}\left\|\bm{\lambda}\right\|_{2}^{2},

being
\bm{\upsilon}\in\mathcal{V}
a generic parameter vector to be optimized. As highlighted in Section
2
, in the case of action-based exploration (
\dagger=\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.4648,0.7344}A}}
)
\bm{\upsilon}
corresponds to the policy parameterization
\bm{\upsilon}=\bm{\theta}\in\Theta
, while in the case of parameter-based exploration (
\dagger=\text{{\color[rgb]{0.8008,0.1992,0.0664}P}}
) it coincides with the hyperpolicy parameterization
\bm{\upsilon}=\bm{\rho}\in\mathcal{R}
. In the following, we are going to consider the gradients w.r.t. both the parameterization
\bm{\upsilon}
and the Lagrange multipliers
\bm{\lambda}
, having the following explicit forms in the exploration-agnostic setting:

\displaystyle\nabla_{\bm{\upsilon}}\mathcal{L}_{\dagger,\omega}(\bm{\upsilon},\bm{\lambda})=\nabla_{\bm{\upsilon}}J_{\dagger,0}(\bm{\upsilon})+\sum_{u=1}^{U}\lambda_{u}\nabla_{\bm{\upsilon}}J_{\dagger,u}(\bm{\upsilon})\quad\text{and}\quad\nabla_{\bm{\lambda}}\mathcal{L}_{\dagger,\omega}(\bm{\upsilon},\bm{\lambda})=\mathbf{J}_{\dagger}(\bm{\upsilon})+\mathbf{b}+\omega\bm{\lambda},

where
\mathbf{J}_{\dagger}(\bm{\upsilon})\coloneqq(J_{\dagger,1}(\bm{\upsilon}),\ldots,J_{\dagger,U}(\bm{\upsilon}))^{\top}
and
\mathbf{b}\coloneqq(b_{1},\ldots,b_{U})^{\top}
.

:
Summary of the sample complexity results of
C-PG
when either keeping
\omega
fixed or setting it as
\omega=\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)

.

Action-based
Exploration for
C-PG

.

The action-based variant of
C-PG
, referred to as
C-PGAE
, aims at optimizing the parameters
\bm{\theta}
of a parametric stochastic policy
\pi_{\bm{\theta}}
. In particular, for every
i\in\mathopen{[\![}0,U\mathclose{]\!]}
, we recall the definition of action-based cost functions:
{\color[rgb]{0,0.4648,0.7344}J_{\text{A},i}}(\bm{\theta})=\E_{\tau\sim p_{\text{A}}(\cdot|\bm{\theta})}\left[C_{i}(\tau)\right]
, where
p_{\text{A}}(\tau,\bm{\theta})
is the probability density of trajectory
\tau
induced by the policy
\pi_{\bm{\theta}}
.

Considering the gradient w.r.t. the parameters
\bm{\theta}
, the following holds:

\displaystyle\nabla_{\bm{\theta}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4648,0.7344}J_{\text{A},i}}(\bm{\theta})

\displaystyle=\nabla_{\bm{\theta}}\E_{\tau\sim p_{\text{A}}(\cdot|\bm{\theta})}\left[C_{i}(\tau)\right]=\E_{\tau\sim p_{\text{A}}(\cdot\mid\bm{\theta})}\left[\nabla_{\bm{\theta}}\log p_{\text{A}}(\tau,\bm{\theta})C_{i}(\tau)\right].

As for standard PGs
(
Williams 1992
;
Baxter and Bartlett 2001
)
, we can switch to its sample-based version to obtain an unbiased estimator of the gradient. In particular, we resort to a GPOMDP-like version
(
Baxter and Bartlett 2001
)
for the proposed estimator:

\displaystyle\widehat{\nabla}_{\bm{\theta}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4648,0.7344}J_{\text{A},i}}(\bm{\theta})\coloneqq\frac{1}{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\left(\sum_{l=0}^{t}\nabla_{\bm{\theta}}\log\pi_{\bm{\theta}}(\bm{a}_{\tau_{j},l},\bm{s}_{\tau_{j},l})\right)\gamma^{t}c_{i}(\bm{s}_{\tau_{j},t},\bm{a}_{\tau_{j},t}),

where
N
, called batch size, is the number of independent trajectories
\{\tau_{j}\}_{j=1}^{N}
such that
\tau_{j}\sim p_{\text{A}}(\cdot\mid\bm{\theta})
. We just consider a GPOMDP-like version of the estimator, since the REINFORCE-like one would suffer from a higher variance as for standard PGs
(
Papini et al. 2022
)
. Thus, considering that:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.4648,0.7344}\mathcal{L}_{\text{A},\omega}}(\bm{\theta},\bm{\lambda})={\color[rgb]{0,0.4648,0.7344}J_{\text{A},0}}(\bm{\theta})+\sum_{u=1}^{U}\lambda_{u}\left({\color[rgb]{0,0.4648,0.7344}J_{\text{A},i}}(\bm{\theta})-b_{u}\right)-\frac{\omega}{2}\left\|\bm{\lambda}\right\|_{2}^{2},

C-PGAE
employs the following estimator to update the primal variable
\bm{\theta}
:

\displaystyle\widehat{\nabla}_{\bm{\theta}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4648,0.7344}\mathcal{L}_{\text{A},\omega}}(\bm{\theta},\bm{\lambda})=\widehat{\nabla}_{\bm{\theta}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4648,0.7344}J_{\text{A},0}}(\bm{\theta})+\sum_{u=1}^{U}\lambda_{u}\widehat{\nabla}_{\bm{\theta}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4648,0.7344}J_{\text{A},i}}(\bm{\theta}).

If we now focus on the action-based gradient w.r.t. Lagrange multipliers, we have the following:

\displaystyle\nabla_{\bm{\lambda}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4648,0.7344}\mathcal{L}_{\text{A},\omega}}(\bm{\theta},\bm{\lambda})={\color[rgb]{0,0.4648,0.7344}\mathbf{J}_{\text{A}}}(\bm{\theta})-\mathbf{b}-\omega\bm{\lambda},

where
{\color[rgb]{0,0.4648,0.7344}\mathbf{J}_{\text{A}}}(\bm{\theta})\coloneqq({\color[rgb]{0,0.4648,0.7344}J_{\text{A},1}}(\bm{\theta}),\ldots,{\color[rgb]{0,0.4648,0.7344}J_{\text{A},U}}(\bm{\theta}))^{\top}
. Thus, its sample-based version used by
C-PGAE
to update the dual variable is the following:

\displaystyle\frac{\widehat{\partial}}{\partial\lambda_{i}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4648,0.7344}\mathcal{L}_{\text{A},\omega}}(\bm{\theta},\bm{\lambda})=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N}C_{i}(\tau_{j})-b_{i}-\omega\lambda_{i},

where the
N
independent trajectories
\{\tau_{j}\}_{j=1}^{N}
are such that
\tau_{j}\sim p_{\text{A}}(\cdot\mid\bm{\theta})
.

Parameter-based
Exploration for
C-PG

.

The parameter-based variant of
C-PG
, referred to as
C-PGPE
, aims at optimizing the parameters
\bm{\rho}
of a parametric stochastic hyperpolicy
\nu_{\bm{\rho}}
, used to sample the parameters
\bm{\theta}
for an underlying parametric policy
\pi_{\bm{\theta}}
(which can also be deterministic, as we shall see in the next section). In particular, for every
i\in\mathopen{[\![}0,U\mathclose{]\!]}
, we recall the definition of parameter-based cost functions:
{\color[rgb]{0.8008,0.1992,0.0664}J_{\text{P},i}}(\bm{\rho})=\E_{\bm{\theta}\sim\nu_{\bm{\rho}}}\left[\E_{\tau\sim p_{\text{A}}(\cdot|\bm{\theta})}\left[C_{i}(\tau)\right]\right]
, where
p_{\text{A}}(\tau,\bm{\theta})
is the probability density of trajectory
\tau
induced by the policy
\pi_{\bm{\theta}}
.

Considering the gradient w.r.t. the parameters
\bm{\theta}
, the following holds:

\displaystyle\nabla_{\bm{\rho}}{\color[rgb]{0.8008,0.1992,0.0664}J_{\text{P},i}}(\bm{\rho})

\displaystyle=\nabla_{\bm{\rho}}\E_{\bm{\theta}\sim\nu_{\bm{\rho}}}\left[\E_{\tau\sim p_{\text{A}}(\cdot|\bm{\theta})}\left[C_{i}(\tau)\right]\right]=\E_{\bm{\theta}\sim\nu_{\bm{\rho}}}\left[\nabla_{\bm{\rho}}\log\nu_{\bm{\rho}}(\bm{\theta})\E_{\tau\sim p_{\text{A}}(\cdot,\bm{\theta})}\left[\nabla_{\bm{\theta}}\log p_{\text{A}}(\tau|\bm{\theta})C_{i}(\tau)\right]\right].

As for the prototypical parameter-based method PGPE
(
Sehnke et al. 2010
)
, we switch to its sample-based version to obtain an unbiased estimator of the gradient:

\displaystyle\widehat{\nabla}_{\bm{\rho}}{\color[rgb]{0.8008,0.1992,0.0664}J_{\text{P},i}}(\bm{\rho})\coloneqq\frac{1}{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\nabla_{\bm{\rho}}\log\nu_{\bm{\rho}}(\bm{\theta}_{j})C_{i}(\tau_{j}),

where
N
, called batch size, is the number of independent parameter-trajectory pairs
\{(\bm{\theta}_{j},\tau_{j})\}_{j=1}^{N}
such that
\tau_{j}\sim p_{\text{A}}(\cdot\mid\bm{\theta}_{j})
and
\bm{\theta}_{j}\sim\nu_{\bm{\rho}}
. We highlight that, for parameter-based exploration, we sample
N
policy parameterizations
\bm{\theta}_{j}
from the hyperpolicy
\nu_{\bm{\rho}}
, for each of which we sample a single trajectory
\tau_{j}\sim p_{\text{A}}(\cdot\mid\bm{\theta}_{j})
. That being said, considering that:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.8008,0.1992,0.0664}\mathcal{L}_{\text{P},\omega}}(\bm{\rho},\bm{\lambda})={\color[rgb]{0.8008,0.1992,0.0664}J_{\text{P},0}}(\bm{\rho})+\sum_{u=1}^{U}\lambda_{u}\left({\color[rgb]{0.8008,0.1992,0.0664}J_{\text{P},i}}(\bm{\rho})-b_{u}\right)-\frac{\omega}{2}\left\|\bm{\lambda}\right\|_{2}^{2},

C-PGPE
employs the following estimator to update the primal variable
\bm{\rho}
:

\displaystyle\widehat{\nabla}_{\bm{\rho}}{\color[rgb]{0.8008,0.1992,0.0664}\mathcal{L}_{\text{P},\omega}}(\bm{\rho},\bm{\lambda})=\widehat{\nabla}_{\bm{\rho}}{\color[rgb]{0.8008,0.1992,0.0664}J_{\text{P},0}}(\bm{\rho})+\sum_{u=1}^{U}\lambda_{u}\widehat{\nabla}_{\bm{\rho}}{\color[rgb]{0.8008,0.1992,0.0664}J_{\text{P},i}}(\bm{\rho}).

If we now focus on the parameter-based gradient w.r.t. Lagrange multipliers, we have the following:

\displaystyle\nabla_{\bm{\lambda}}{\color[rgb]{0.8008,0.1992,0.0664}\mathcal{L}_{\text{P},\omega}}(\bm{\rho},\bm{\lambda})={\color[rgb]{0.8008,0.1992,0.0664}\mathbf{J}_{\text{P}}}(\bm{\rho})-\mathbf{b}-\omega\bm{\lambda},

where
{\color[rgb]{0.8008,0.1992,0.0664}\mathbf{J}_{\text{P}}}(\bm{\rho})\coloneqq({\color[rgb]{0.8008,0.1992,0.0664}J_{\text{P},1}}(\bm{\rho}),\ldots,{\color[rgb]{0.8008,0.1992,0.0664}J_{\text{P},U}}(\bm{\rho}))^{\top}
. Thus, its sample-based version used by
C-PGPE
to update the dual variable is the following:

\displaystyle\frac{\widehat{\partial}}{\partial\lambda_{i}}{\color[rgb]{0.8008,0.1992,0.0664}\mathcal{L}_{\text{P},\omega}}(\bm{\rho},\bm{\lambda})=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N}C_{i}(\tau_{j})-b_{i}-\omega\lambda_{i},

where the
N
independent trajectories
\{\tau_{j}\}_{j=1}^{N}
are such that
\tau_{j}\sim p_{\text{A}}(\cdot\mid\bm{\theta}_{j})
, where
\bm{\theta}_{j}\sim\nu_{\bm{\rho}}
for every
j\in\mathopen{[\![}N\mathclose{]\!]}
.

4
Deterministic Policy Deployment of
C-PG

In this section, we analyze the convergence guarantees of
C-PG
towards an optimal deterministic policy. To this end, we focus on the setting of
white noise (hyper)policies

(
Montenegro et al. 2024a
)
. Specifically, we restrict both action-based and parameter-based exploration strategies as stochastic perturbations of an underlying parametric deterministic policy
\mu_{\bm{\theta}}
. In this framework, stochastic policies
\pi_{\bm{\theta}}
are modeled as perturbations of the actions prescribed by
\mu_{\bm{\theta}}
, while stochastic hyperpolicies
\nu_{\bm{\rho}}
are interpreted as perturbations of the parameters
\bm{\theta}
of the deterministic policy.
Leveraging this structure, we study the behavior of the
C-PG
algorithm when it learns using stochastic (hyper)policies and subsequently
deploys
their deterministic counterpart by
switching off
the stochasticity at the end of training.
Our analysis establishes the sample complexity required by
C-PG
to guarantee that the deployed deterministic policy, regardless of the exploration paradigm used during training, is an optimal feasible one.

We begin by presenting the noise model used in our analysis (Section
4.1
), followed by a description of the deterministic policy deployment process in CMDPs (Section
4.2
). We then state the assumptions required for convergence (Section
4.3
), and finally provide the sample complexity required by
C-PG
to converge in the last iterate to an optimal deterministic policy when the noise is
switched off
at the end of training (Section
4.4
).

4.1
White Noise Exploration

While deterministic policies are desirable in real-world applications (see Section
1
), learning them directly typically requires off-policy actor-critic architectures
(
Silver et al. 2014
;
Lillicrap et al. 2015
;
Xiong et al. 2022
)
, which pose significant challenges for convergence analysis even in unconstrained settings. In this part, we introduce a specific noise model that enables us to restrict both action-based and parameter-based exploration strategies as stochastic perturbations of deterministic policies. This formulation allows us to quantify the performance gap induced by a given parameterization of a stochastic (hyper)policy w.r.t. its associated deterministic policy.

We begin by defining deterministic policies and the related performance and cost functions. A parametric deterministic policy is a function
\mu_{\bm{\theta}}:\mathcal{S}\rightarrow\mathcal{A},

where
\bm{\theta}\in\Theta\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d_{\Theta}}
is the parameter vector.
For every
i\in\mathopen{[\![}0,U\mathclose{]\!]}
, the performance and cost functions
J_{\text{D},i}:\Theta\to\mathbb{R}
related to a deterministic policy are:

\displaystyle J_{\text{D},i}(\bm{\theta})\coloneqq\E_{\tau\sim p_{\text{D}}(\cdot|\bm{\theta})}\left[C_{i}(\tau)\right],

where
p_{\text{D}}(\tau;\bm{\theta})\coloneqq\phi_{0}(\bm{s}_{0})\prod_{t=0}^{T-1}p(\bm{s}_{t+1}|\bm{s}_{t},\mu_{\bm{\theta}}(\bm{s}_{t}))
is the probability density of trajectory
\tau
induced by
\mu_{\bm{\theta}}
. Using these definitions, we introduce the deterministic regularized Lagrangian function employed by
C-PG
as:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\text{D},\omega}(\bm{\theta},\bm{\lambda})=J_{\text{D},0}(\bm{\theta})+\sum_{i=1}^{U}\left(\lambda_{i}(J_{\text{D},i}(\bm{\theta})-b_{i})\right)-\frac{\omega}{2}\|\bm{\bm{\lambda}}\|_{2}^{2}.

We can now redefine both
AB
and
PB
exploration on top of deterministic policies
(
Montenegro et al. 2024a
)
. Considering
AB
exploration, stochasticity is injected at the action level, perturbing the deterministic policy’s output at each environmental interaction step. In
PB
exploration, noise is applied directly to the policy parameters before execution, resulting in a fixed perturbed version of the underlying deterministic policy for an entire trajectory. Next, we formally present how we intend a perturbation in the action or parameter spaces.

Definition 4.1

(White Noise)
.

Let
d\in\mathbb{N}
and
\sigma\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
. A probability distribution
\Phi_{d}\in\Delta\left(\mathbb{R}^{d}\right)
is defined as
white noise
if it satisfies the following conditions:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\bm{\epsilon}\sim\Phi_{d}}[\bm{\epsilon}]=\bm{0}_{d},\quad\mathbb{E}_{\bm{\epsilon}\sim\Phi_{d}}[\left\|\bm{\epsilon}\right\|_{2}^{2}]\leq d\sigma^{2},

(7)

where
\bm{0}_{d}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
is a
d
-dimensional vector of all zero components.

Definition
4.1
includes zero-mean Gaussian distributions
\bm{\epsilon}\sim\mathcal{N}(\bm{0}_{d},\sigma\bm{\Lambda})
with
\lambda_{\max}(\bm{\Lambda})=1
, ensuring that
\mathbb{E}[\|\bm{\epsilon}\|_{2}^{2}]=\sigma^{2}\text{tr}(\bm{\Lambda})\leq d\sigma^{2}
. We stress that this noise has to be considered
white
across exploration steps. We can now redefine action-based and parameter-based explorations as white noise perturbations of the actions or the parameters of an underlying parametric deterministic policy
\mu_{\bm{\theta}}
.

Action-Based
(
AB
) Exploration.

Considering
AB
PG methods (see Section
2
), we consider a
parametric stochastic policy

\pi_{\bm{\theta}}
as built upon an underlying deterministic policy
\mu_{\bm{\theta}}
by perturbing each action suggested by
\mu_{\bm{\theta}}
with a white noise random vector. Formally, we consider the following definition of white noise policies.

Definition 4.2

(
White Noise Policies
)
.

Let
\bm{\theta}\in\Theta
and
\mu_{\bm{\theta}}:\mathcal{S}\to\mathcal{A}
be a parametric deterministic policy. Given a white noise distribution
\Phi_{d_{\mathcal{A}}}
(Definition
4.1
), a white-noise-based policy
\pi_{\bm{\theta}}:\mathcal{S}\rightarrow\Delta(\mathcal{A})
is defined such that, for every state
\bm{s}\in\mathcal{S}
, the action
\bm{a}\sim\pi_{\bm{\theta}}(\cdot|\bm{s})
satisfies
\bm{a}=\mu_{\bm{\theta}}(\bm{s})+\bm{\epsilon}
, where
\bm{\epsilon}\sim\Phi_{d_{\mathcal{A}}}
which is sampled independently at every step (i.e., whenever an action is sampled).

We highlight that Definition
4.2
further justifies the name for
AB
exploration since the exploration is carried out at the action level.

Next, for every
i\in\mathopen{[\![}0,U\mathclose{]\!]}
we redefine the cost functions
{\color[rgb]{0,0.4648,0.7344}J_{\text{A},i}}
leveraging the introduced characterization of white noise policies.
To this end, we need to introduce the concept of
non stationary
deterministic cost functions. Let
\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}=(\bm{\epsilon}_{t})_{t=0}^{T-1}
be a sequence of independently sampled white noise vectors satisfying Definition
4.1
. Let
\underline{\bm{\mu}}=(\bm{\mu}_{t})_{t=0}^{T-1}
be a non stationary deterministic policy where, at time step
t
, the deterministic policy
\bm{\mu}_{t}:\mathcal{S}\to\mathcal{A}
is played, with
\bm{\mu}_{t}=\mu_{\bm{\theta}}+\bm{\epsilon}_{t}
. For every
i\in\mathopen{[\![}0,U\mathclose{]\!]}
, we introduce the cost functions for this kind of policy:
J_{\text{D},i}(\underline{\bm{\mu}})\coloneqq\E_{\tau\sim p_{\text{D}}(\cdot\mid\underline{\bm{\mu}})}[C_{i}(\tau)]
, where
p_{\text{D}}(\tau,\underline{\bm{\mu}})
is the density of a trajectory
\tau
induced by the non stationary deterministic policy
\underline{\bm{\mu}}
.
Equipped with this new definition, we can reintroduce the
AB
cost functions
{\color[rgb]{0,0.4648,0.7344}J_{\text{A},i}}
which admit the following definition, together with the one already provided in Section
2
, when the considered stochastic policy
\pi_{\bm{\theta}}
complies with Definition
4.2
:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.4648,0.7344}J_{\text{A},i}}(\bm{\theta})\coloneqq\E_{\bm{\epsilon}\sim\Phi_{d_{\mathcal{A}}}^{T}}\left[J_{\text{D},i}(\underline{\bm{\mu}}_{\bm{\theta}}+\underline{\bm{\epsilon}})\right],

where
\underline{\bm{\mu}}_{\bm{\theta}}+\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}=(\mu_{\bm{\theta}}+\bm{\epsilon}_{t})_{t=0}^{T-1}
and
\Phi_{d_{\mathcal{A}}}
is a white noise distribution compliant with Definition
4.1
..

Parameter-Based
(
PB
) Exploration.

Considering
PB
PG methods (see Section
2
), we consider a
parametric stochastic hyperpolicy

\nu_{\bm{\theta}}
as built upon an underlying deterministic policy
\mu_{\bm{\theta}}
by perturbing the parameter vector
\bm{\theta}
with a white noise random vector. Formally, we consider the following definition of white-noise hyperpolicies.

Definition 4.3

(
White Noise Hyperpolicies
)
.

Let
\bm{\theta}\in\Theta
and
\mu_{\bm{\theta}}:\mathcal{S}\to\mathcal{A}
be a parametric deterministic policy. Given a white noise distribution
\Phi_{d_{\Theta}}
(Definition
4.1
), a white-noise-based hyperpolicy
\nu_{\bm{\theta}}\in\Delta(\Theta)
is defined such that, for every parameter
\bm{\theta}\in\Theta
, the perturbed parameter
\bm{\theta}^{\prime}\sim\nu_{\bm{\theta}}
satisfies
\bm{\theta}^{\prime}=\bm{\theta}+\bm{\epsilon}
, where
\bm{\epsilon}\sim\Phi_{d_{\Theta}}
, independently for every trajectory.

As previously done for action-based exploration, we stress that this definition further justifies the name of
PB
exploration, since the exploration is carried out at parameter level.
Moreover, we let the reader note that the noise
\bm{\epsilon}
is sampled once at the
beginning
of each trajectory, meaning that the resulting policy
\mu_{\bm{\theta}+\bm{\epsilon}}
remains deterministic throughout the entire trajectory collection phase.

Next, for every
i\in\mathopen{[\![}0,U\mathclose{]\!]}
we redefine the cost functions
{\color[rgb]{0.8008,0.1992,0.0664}J_{\text{P},i}}
leveraging the introduced characterization of white noise hyperpolicies.
We reintroduce the
PB
cost functions, which admit the following definition, together with the one already provided in Section
2
, when the considered stochastic hyperpolicy
\nu_{\bm{\theta}}
complies with Definition
4.3
:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.8008,0.1992,0.0664}J_{\text{P},i}}(\bm{\theta})=\E_{\bm{\epsilon}\sim\Phi_{d_{\Theta}}}\left[J_{\text{D},i}(\bm{\theta}+\bm{\epsilon})\right],

where
\Phi_{d_{\Theta}}
is a white noise distribution compliant with Definition
4.1
.

In the remaining part of this section, we will consider an
exploration-agnostic
setting, in which we denote with
\dagger\in\{{\color[rgb]{0,0.4648,0.7344}\text{A}},{\color[rgb]{0.8008,0.1992,0.0664}\text{P}}\}
the two different exploration approaches. We highlight that the problem formulation and all the theoretical results of Section
3
still hold.

4.2
Deploying Deterministic Policies in CMDPs

In this part, we analyze the effect of switching off the stochasticity on the regularized Lagrangian employed by
C-PG
to solve the COP in Equation (
1
) when dealing with stochastic policies and hyperpolicies that satisfy Definitions
4.2
and
4.3
, respectively.

Before presenting such results, we introduce two assumptions enforcing the regularity of the deterministic objectives
J_{\text{D},i}
w.r.t. the parameters
\bm{\theta}
and the non stationary deterministic policies
\underline{\bm{\mu}}
associated with
AB
exploration.

Assumption 4.1

(
J_{\text{D},i}
Regularity w.r.t.
\bm{\theta}
)
.

For every
i\in\mathopen{[\![}0,U\mathclose{]\!]}
, there exist
L_{1\text{D},i},L_{2\text{D},i}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
such that, for every
\bm{\theta},\bm{\theta}^{\prime}\in\Theta
, the following conditions hold:

\displaystyle\left|J_{\text{D},i}(\bm{\theta})-J_{\text{D},i}(\bm{\theta}^{\prime})\right|\leq L_{1\text{D},i}\left\|\bm{\theta}-\bm{\theta}^{\prime}\right\|_{2}\quad\text{and}\quad\left\|\nabla_{\bm{\theta}}J_{\text{D},i}(\bm{\theta})-\nabla_{\bm{\theta}}J_{\text{D},i}(\bm{\theta}^{\prime})\right\|_{2}\leq L_{2\text{D},i}\left\|\bm{\theta}-\bm{\theta}^{\prime}\right\|_{2}.

Moreover, we denote
L_{1\text{D},\max}\coloneqq\max_{i\in\mathopen{[\![}0,U\mathclose{]\!]}}L_{1\text{D},i}
and
L_{2\text{D},\max}\coloneqq\max_{i\in\mathopen{[\![}0,U\mathclose{]\!]}}L_{2\text{D},i}
.

Assumption 4.2

(
J_{\text{D},i}
Regularity w.r.t.
\underline{\bm{\mu}}
)
.

For every
i\in\mathopen{[\![}0,U\mathclose{]\!]}
, there exist
L_{1\underline{\bm{\mu}},i},L_{2\underline{\bm{\mu}},i}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
such that, for every pair of non stationary deterministic policies
\underline{\bm{\mu}},\underline{\bm{\mu}}^{\prime}
, the following conditions hold:

\displaystyle\left|J_{\text{D},i}(\underline{\bm{\mu}})-J_{\text{D},i}(\underline{\bm{\mu}}^{\prime})\right|\leq L_{1\underline{\bm{\mu}},i}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\sup_{\bm{s}\in\mathcal{S}}\left\|\bm{\mu}_{t}(\bm{s})-\bm{\mu}_{t}^{\prime}(\bm{s})\right\|_{2}\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla_{\underline{\bm{\mu}}}J_{\text{D},i}(\underline{\bm{\mu}})-\nabla_{\underline{\bm{\mu}}}J_{\text{D},i}(\underline{\bm{\mu}}^{\prime})\right\|_{2}\leq L_{2\underline{\bm{\mu}},i}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\sup_{\bm{s}\in\mathcal{S}}\left\|\bm{\mu}_{t}(\bm{s})-\bm{\mu}_{t}^{\prime}(\bm{s})\right\|_{2}.

Moreover, we denote
L_{1\underline{\bm{\mu}},\max}\coloneqq\max_{i\in\mathopen{[\![}0,U\mathclose{]\!]}}L_{1\underline{\bm{\mu}},i}
and
L_{2\underline{\bm{\mu}},\max}\coloneqq\max_{i\in\mathopen{[\![}0,U\mathclose{]\!]}}L_{2\underline{\bm{\mu}},i}
.

We stress that these assumptions will be crucial for presenting the core result of this section regarding the effects on
\mathcal{L}_{\dagger,\omega}
when switching off the stochasticity in the context of white noise exploration (see Section
4.1
). Additionally, we let the reader note that Assumption
4.1
induces both
{\color[rgb]{0,0.4648,0.7344}J_{\text{A},i}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.8008,0.1992,0.0664}J_{\text{P},i}}
to enjoy the same regularity condition stated in such an assumption when considering (hyper)policies complying with Definitions
4.2
and
4.3

(
Montenegro et al. 2024a
)
. Moreover, the Lipschitz constants are fully characterized in
(
Montenegro et al. 2024a
)
.

We are now ready to analyze the effect of switching off the stochasticity in
PB
and
AB
exploration on the regularized Lagrangian
\mathcal{L}_{\dagger,\omega}
employed by the
C-PG
method.

Theorem 4.1
.

Considering (hyper)policies complying with Definitions
4.2
(
AB
) or
4.3
(
PB
), under Assumptions
4.1
(
PB
) or
4.2
(
AB
), the following results hold:

i.

(Uniform Bound) for every
\bm{\theta}\in\Theta
and
\bm{\lambda}\in\mathbb{R}^{U}_{\geq 0}
:

\left|\mathcal{L}_{\text{D},\omega}(\bm{\theta},\bm{\lambda})-\mathcal{L}_{\dagger,\omega}(\bm{\theta},\bm{\lambda})\right|\leq\left(1+\left\|\bm{\lambda}\right\|_{1}\right)L_{1\dagger}\sigma\sqrt{d_{\dagger}}.

ii.

(
\mathcal{L}_{\text{D},\omega}
Upper Bound) let
(\bm{\theta}_{\text{D},\omega}^{*},\bm{\lambda}_{\text{D},\omega}^{*})
be a saddle point of
\mathcal{L}_{\text{D},\omega}
and let
(\bm{\theta}^{*}_{\dagger,\omega},\bm{\lambda}^{*}_{\dagger,\omega})
be a saddle point of
\mathcal{L}_{\dagger,\omega}
. Then:

\mathcal{L}_{\text{D},\omega}(\bm{\theta}^{*}_{\dagger,\omega},\bm{\lambda}^{*}_{\dagger,\omega})-\mathcal{L}_{\text{D},\omega}(\bm{\theta}_{\text{D},\omega}^{*},\bm{\lambda}_{\text{D},\omega}^{*})\leq 2\left(1+\|\bm{\lambda}^{*}_{\dagger,\omega}\|_{1}\right)L_{1\dagger}\sigma\sqrt{d_{\dagger}}.

Where
L_{1\text{{\color[rgb]{0.8008,0.1992,0.0664}P}}}\coloneqq L_{1\text{D},\max}
,
L_{1\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.4648,0.7344}A}}}\coloneqq L_{1\underline{\bm{\mu}},\max}
,
d_{\text{{\color[rgb]{0.8008,0.1992,0.0664}P}}}\coloneqq d_{\Theta}
, and
d_{\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.4648,0.7344}A}}}\coloneqq d_{\mathcal{A}}
.

Proof.

We start the derivation by recalling the explicit form of
\left|\mathcal{L}_{\text{D},\omega}(\bm{\theta},\bm{\lambda})-\mathcal{L}_{\dagger,\omega}(\bm{\theta},\bm{\lambda})\right|
:

\displaystyle\left|\mathcal{L}_{\text{D},\omega}(\bm{\theta},\bm{\lambda})-\mathcal{L}_{\dagger,\omega}(\bm{\theta},\bm{\lambda})\right|

\displaystyle=\left|J_{\text{D},0}(\bm{\theta})+\sum_{i=1}^{U}\lambda_{i}\left(J_{\text{D},i}(\bm{\theta})-b_{i}\right)-\frac{\omega}{2}\left\|\bm{\lambda}\right\|_{2}^{2}-J_{\dagger,0}(\bm{\theta})-\sum_{i=1}^{U}\lambda_{i}\left(J_{\dagger,i}(\bm{\theta})-b_{i}\right)+\frac{\omega}{2}\left\|\bm{\lambda}\right\|_{2}^{2}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left|J_{\text{D},0}(\bm{\theta})-J_{\dagger,0}(\bm{\theta})\right|+\sum_{i=1}^{U}\lambda_{i}\left|J_{\text{D},i}(\bm{\theta})-J_{\dagger,i}(\bm{\theta})\right|,

where the last line follows by simply having applied the triangular inequality.

To continue the proof, we need to resort to Theorems 5.1 (
PB
) and 5.2 (
AB
) by
(
Montenegro et al. 2024a
)
. These state that under Assumptions
4.1
(
PB
) or
4.2
(
AB
), when dealing with an (hyper)policy complying with Definitions
4.2
(
AB
) and
4.3
(
PB
), the following holds:

\displaystyle\left|J_{\text{D},i}(\bm{\theta})-J_{\dagger,i}(\bm{\theta})\right|\leq L_{1\dagger,i}\sigma\sqrt{d_{\dagger}},

where
L_{1\text{{\color[rgb]{0.8008,0.1992,0.0664}P}},i}\coloneqq L_{1\text{D},i}
,
L_{1\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.4648,0.7344}A}},i}\coloneqq L_{1\underline{\bm{\mu}},i}
,
d_{\text{{\color[rgb]{0.8008,0.1992,0.0664}P}}}=d_{\Theta}
, and
d_{\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.4648,0.7344}A}}}=d_{\mathcal{A}}
.

By leveraging this result, the following holds:

\displaystyle\left|\mathcal{L}_{\text{D},\omega}(\bm{\theta},\bm{\lambda})-\mathcal{L}_{\dagger,\omega}(\bm{\theta},\bm{\lambda})\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left|J_{\text{D},0}(\bm{\theta})-J_{\dagger,0}(\bm{\theta})\right|+\sum_{i=1}^{U}\lambda_{i}\left|J_{\text{D},i}(\bm{\theta})-J_{\dagger,i}(\bm{\theta})\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left(1+\sum_{i=1}^{U}\lambda_{i}\right)L_{1\dagger}\sigma\sqrt{d_{\dagger}}

\displaystyle=\left(1+\left\|\bm{\lambda}\right\|_{1}\right)L_{1\dagger}\sigma\sqrt{d_{\dagger}},

being
L_{1\text{{\color[rgb]{0.8008,0.1992,0.0664}P}}}\coloneqq L_{1\text{D},\max}
and
L_{1\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.4648,0.7344}A}}}\coloneqq L_{1\underline{\bm{\mu}},\max}
, which concludes the first part of the proof.

We can now face the second part of the proof. In particular, let
(\bm{\theta}^{*}_{\dagger,\omega},\bm{\lambda}^{*}_{\dagger,\omega})
be a saddle point of
\mathcal{L}_{\dagger,\omega}
and let
(\bm{\theta}^{*}_{\text{D},\omega},\bm{\lambda}^{*}_{\text{D},\omega})
be a saddle point of
\mathcal{L}_{\text{D},\omega}
. Before going on with the derivation, we recall that a saddle point by definition satisfies the following property:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\text{D},\omega}(\bm{\theta}^{*}_{\text{D},\omega},\bm{\lambda})\leq\mathcal{L}_{\text{D},\omega}(\bm{\theta}^{*}_{\text{D},\omega},\bm{\lambda}^{*}_{\text{D},\omega})\leq\mathcal{L}_{\text{D},\omega}(\bm{\theta},\bm{\lambda}^{*}_{\text{D},\omega}),

for every
\bm{\theta}\in\Theta
and
\bm{\lambda}\in\mathbb{R}^{U}_{\geq 0}
. That being said, the following holds:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\text{D},\omega}(\bm{\theta}^{*}_{\dagger,\omega},\bm{\lambda}^{*}_{\dagger,\omega})-\mathcal{L}_{\text{D},\omega}(\bm{\theta}_{\text{D},\omega}^{*},\bm{\lambda}_{\text{D},\omega}^{*})

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{L}_{\text{D},\omega}(\bm{\theta}^{*}_{\dagger,\omega},\bm{\lambda}^{*}_{\dagger,\omega})-\mathcal{L}_{\text{D},\omega}(\bm{\theta}_{\text{D},\omega}^{*},\bm{\lambda}^{*}_{\dagger,\omega})
